 D.4. Climate vulnerability

 Programme for Southeast Asia (EFPSEA) ranks Lao PDR as one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the world.

<doc> • The current capabilities of government staff at all levels to actively integrate gender and ethnic considerations 
 in their work are limited, and there is a need for more training on how to do so effectively. This will be achieved through the provision of 
 Ethnic language training and other capacity building activities. The project will also include the development of guidelines and tools that 
 will facilitate the inclusion of women’s voices in decision-making processes at all levels, including the development of a gender 
 and traditional norms (ETN) language guide for use by government staff. 
 • Linguistically, women and men have the same rights to land, but rarely participate in forest management activities that affect 
 their livelihoods. The project will provide training on how to do so effectively. This will be achieved through the provision of 
 Ethnic language training and other capacity building activities. The project will also include the development of guidelines and tools that 
 will facilitate the inclusion of women’s voices in decision-making processes at all levels, including the development of a gender 
 and traditional norms (ETN) language guide for use by government staff. 
 • Legally, women and men have the same rights to land, but rarely participate in forest management activities that affect their livelihoods. The project will provide training on how to do so effectively. This will be achieved through the provision of

# No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

## Lao People's Democratic Republic
Peace Independence Democracy Unity Prosperity
Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment
Department of Climate Change
Vientiane, 25 February 2019

## To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

## Subject: No-Objection Letter - Funding proposal for the GCF by GIZ regarding "Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management"

Dear Mr. Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project "Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management" (hereinafter referred to as the project). The funding proposal submitted by GIZ on 12th February 2019 is included in this letter.

## Background
The Government of Lao People's Democratic Republic has been actively working towards reducing greenhouse gas emissions and promoting sustainable development. In line with this objective, the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment has initiated a project to improve governance and promote sustainable forest landscape management in the country.

## Project Description
The project aims to enhance the capacity of government agencies and local communities to implement effective policies and programs for reducing greenhouse gas emissions and promoting sustainable development. The project will focus on improving governance, enhancing transparency and accountability, and promoting sustainable forest management practices.

## No-Objection Letter
We hereby declare that the proposed funding proposal by GIZ is in compliance with the national priorities, strategies, and plans of the Government of Lao People's Democratic Republic. The project is also in line with relevant national laws and regulations.

## Conclusion
We confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project has been duly followed. We also confirm that the proposed funding proposal by GIZ is in compliance with the national priorities, strategies, and plans of the Government of Lao People's Democratic Republic. The project is also in line with relevant national laws and regulations.

## Acknowledgement
We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Sincerely,

Syamphone Sengchandala
National Designated Authority of Lao PDR

# Determined Contributions (NDCI), the NSEDP targets in terms of reducing deforestation and increasing the national forest cover. Therefore, it is of utmost importance for MAF to prioritize this proposal and bring it to the 25th GCF Board Meeting in July 2019.

## For this reason, we would like to request your kind consideration to notify us no objection for this funding proposal to GCF Secretariat in due time.

## Director General, Department of Forestry

# Gender Assessment

## Supporting a Funding Proposal to the Green Climate Fund

### Programme Title: Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management

### Commissioned by Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH


---
**Page 139**
---

1. INTRODUCTION


---
**Page 140**
---

# List of Abbreviations


---
**Page 141**
---

1. Introduction

Lao PDR is a landlocked, Least Developed Country (LLDC) with dwindling forest cover, from 70% in the 1950s to 58% in 2015. Forests are not only an important sector for Lao national economy but are also central to the country's culture and identity. However, deforestation has been a major problem since the 1960s due to poverty, lack of awareness on the natural products derived from lana and forests. Forest degradation and deforestation therefore pose a significant risk to the livelihoods of a majority of the Lao population. Women, the poor, and geographically remote communities are particularly vulnerable to this problem.

The proposed programme aims to support the successful implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management. The programme will address barriers at national and sub-national levels, including development planning policy and regulators environment, law enforcement, measurement, reporting and verification (MRV), monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) and stakeholder engagement.

Output 1 will address barriers at the national and sub-national levels, including development planning policy and regulators environment, law enforcement, measurement, reporting and verification (MRV), monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) and stakeholder engagement. Output 2 will address key drivers of deforestation and degradation, it delivers emission reduction targets for the forest sector, and it promotes sustainable forest management practices in Lao PDR.

Output 3 aims to reduce emissions through Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) and forest conservation activities, including the enhancement of carbon sequestration through afforestation/reforestation, rewilding and restoration of degraded lands, and the promotion of sustainable use of natural resources.

Output 4 includes management activities and other services, as well as a contingency fund. The Gender Action Plan and stakeholder engagement activities are also planned under this output.

The data should be collected from a variety of sources, including government agencies, non-governmental organizations, and community groups.
2. The assessment will also consider the context in which gender equality is being addressed. This includes considering factors such as cultural norms, political ideologies, and economic conditions that may impact the implementation of gender equality programs.
3. The results of the assessment will be used to inform the development of gender-sensitive policies and programs. This includes identifying areas where gender equality is lacking and developing strategies to address these gaps. The assessment will also provide recommendations for how to improve the effectiveness of existing gender equality programs.
4. The assessment will be conducted in a transparent and accountable manner, with input from relevant stakeholders such as government agencies, non-governmental organizations, and community groups. This includes ensuring that the results of the assessment are communicated clearly and effectively to all stakeholders, and that any recommendations for improving gender equality programs are implemented in a timely and effective manner.
5. The assessment will be conducted in accordance with international standards and best practices for gender equality assessments. This includes using standardized data collection methods and analysis techniques, as well as ensuring that the results of the assessment are reported in a clear and concise manner.

1. The availability of recent data and information on gender in the forest sector is limited in both provinces due to a lack of awareness of larger and more generic country assessments of donors, and census data collection.
2. The field consultations took place between 15-24th January 2013, one mission to Houaghan Province from 15-18th January, and one mission to Luang Namtha Province from 22-24th January 2013. In both Provinces, interviews were conducted with the Provincial Agriculture Officers (OAO) and District Laws Women’s Union (DLWU) in Xam Neu (Houaghan) and Luang Namtha Province from Xam Neu (Houaghan) and Luang Namtha District.
3. At the District level, interviews were conducted with the District Agriculture and Forestry Offices (OAO) and District Laws Women’s Union (DLWU) in Xam Neu (Houaghan) and Luang Namtha Province from Xam Neu (Houaghan) and Luang Namtha District.
4. In total, 148 people were interviewed and consulted [79 men and 69 women].
5. The selected villages are of different ethnic and economic background, and showed different levels of women’s participation:
* Ban Yard village/Houaghan: Khmu and Hmong ethnicity, with two villages merged into one. High poverty and unemployment rates, low education standards. Strong weaving tradition, livestock as central income activity, shifting cultivation and pastoral use of land.
* Ban Nam Mad Mai village/Luang Namtha: Akhsa ethnicity. Village was moved several times in the past 20 years and has experienced improving income levels since they now have more access to credit and are able to invest in their farms.
* Ban Nam Deo village/Luang Namtha: Lanten/Pao ethnicity. The village is an eco-tourism site. Income levels are proportionally higher, but with high dependence on tourism revenue which will end soon, and on their income from rubber with low profit margins.
* Ban Nam Deo village/Luang Namtha: Lanten/Pao ethnicity. The village is an eco-tourism site. Income levels are proportionally higher, but with high dependence on tourism revenue which will end soon, and on their income from rubber with low profit margins.

# Strategic Plan for Biodiversity and Gender Equality in ASEAN Region

## Introduction

The Strategic Plan for Biodiversity and Gender Equality (SPBG) is a comprehensive plan that aims to integrate gender into related national policies and implementation efforts. The plan is aligned with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and provides concrete tools and action steps to report on actions undertaken in implementing the Gender Plan of Action under the CBD.

## Four Strategic Objectives of the CBD Gender Plan of Action

1. Integration of gender perspectives
2. Promoting gender equality
3. Increasing the effectiveness of implementation efforts
4. Integrating gender aspects into all mentioned spheres relevant to successful implementation of the Convention.

## Regional Agreements on Forestry in the ASEAN Region

The only regional agreement in the forestry and biodiversity sector is a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on ASEAN Cooperation in Agriculture and Forest Products Promotion. The MoU was signed in 2015 by the Ministries of Forestry, Fisheries and Aquaculture, and Environmental Protection of the participating countries.

## Regional Agreements on Forestry in the ASEAN Region

The only regional agreement in the forestry and biodiversity sector is a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on ASEAN Cooperation in Agriculture and Forest Products Promotion. The MoU was signed in 2015 by the Ministries of Forestry, Fisheries and Aquaculture, and Environmental Protection of the participating countries.

1. The document is a report on gender equality in Laos. It discusses the legal framework for gender equality, including the Labor Law, the Family Law, the Law on Women's Union, and the Law on the Development and Protection of Women. The report also mentions the role of the Lao Women's Union in promoting gender equality.
2. The report highlights the importance of integrating gender into all sectors of society, including education, healthcare, and employment. It also emphasizes the need for concrete guidance from government agencies to ensure that gender equality is effectively implemented.
3. The report mentions several initiatives aimed at promoting gender equality in Laos, such as the Gender Inequality Index, which measures the gender gap in various areas of life, and the Lao Women's Union, which advocates for women's rights and empowerment.
4. The report also discusses the challenges faced by women in Laos, including low literacy rates, limited access to education and healthcare, and discrimination in employment. It highlights the need for targeted policies and programs to address these issues.
5. The report concludes that while progress has been made towards gender equality in Laos, there is still much work to be done to ensure that all women have equal opportunities and rights. It calls for continued efforts from government agencies, civil society organizations, and the private sector to promote gender equality and empower women.

1. The Meta Level: Norms and Traditional Roles of Women and Men in Laos
2. Despite a strong legal framework stating and promoting the equality of Lao women and men, the influence of gender norms and traditional roles is still as severe as ever of the major destructors in achieving factual gender equality in Laos. This becomes most visible in decision-making positions throughout all sectors, as well as at the community level where women continue to struggle to participate on equal terms and in equal numbers.
3. Gender equality is additionally influenced by ethnic background in Laos. The Lao Tai group represents 67% of Lao PDR's population, along with three major non-Lao-Tai ethno-linguistic groups (Khmou, Hmong and Chinese "Tibetan"). These groups further splinter into 49 distinct ethnicities and 200 ethnic sub-groups. Many traditional norms within Lao-Tai cultures are favorable with regard to gender equality; however, the influence of these norms is not uniform across all regions in Laos.
4. Violence against women is, however, a reality for women from all ethnic backgrounds in Laos. Women are abused by a partner or non-partner, and at least 35% of women live in circumstances of emotional violence, with both figures estimated to be much higher since most cases go unreported. The prevalence of domestic violence is also high among women who have been married for less than five years.
5. Violence against women is justified if a woman does not adhere to traditional gender norms and roles, such as being submissive to her husband or having limited access to education. The other three ethno-linguistic groups mostly have stronger patriarchal traditions and norms, limiting women's access to decision-making positions throughout all sectors in Laos.

# Implications for the Planned GCF Programme

The legal foundation for the promotion of gender mainstreaming into policies and regulations is strong. Laos has an elevated interest in adhering to international agreements relevant to women's rights. The national framework to integrate gender into policies and strategies at all levels is broad and strong. Unfortunately, this strength might at the same time constitute a barrier to further push towards gender equality.

At the regional level, several ASEAN declarations lay the foundations for gender mainstreaming in the region. The Declaration on the Advancement of Women in the ASEAN Region (2015) and the Declaration Against Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children (2004), the Law on the Development and Protection of Women (2001) is the most specific. The Law on the Electoral Law and the Penal Law are also relevant.

The social dimension was added consecutively in later declarations, namely with the ASEAN Declaration Against Trafficking in Persons, Especially Women and Children (2004), the Law on the Development and Protection of Women (2001) is the most specific. The Law on the Electoral Law and the Penal Law are also relevant.

# Lao Front for National Construction (LFWU) Overview

The Lao Front for National Construction (LFWU), also known as the Lao Front, is a political party in Laos that has been active since 1984. The party was founded by General Vientiane and has been led by General Soksan Khong. The LFWU is a member of the United Nations (UN) and is committed to promoting gender equality, including women's rights and development, child protection, support for people with disabilities, education, health, and environmental conservation.

1. The gender equality gap has narrowed at all three levels of education enrollment in Laos, but challenges persist in completing education.
2. Girls are more likely to be kept at home due to safety concerns and household responsibilities, especially if the secondary school is far from home.
3. Secondary parents do not place the same value on education as primary parents or if they are poor, or have little or no education, especially the mother.
4. The wider gap in gender equality is found among children from rural areas without road access, children of uneducated mothers and children of families in the poorest quintiles. This gap is much larger in secondary education, for example, among the youngest age group of the poorest quintiles, 50 girls attend secondary school for every 100 boys.

1. The majority of interviewed village women had no or very low education. This lack of education was named - by men and women alike - as the main reason for the absence of women in decision-making committees and groups in general, and particularly with regard to the villagers' forestry.
2. The shares of women in wage employment is low in all sectors, at 35 percent; instead, among the unpaid workers for the family, about 70% percent were women in 2015, but only 37% are identified as "own account workers" - which suggests that women are less likely engaged in paid work.
3. The majority of village women and men agreed that women work longer hours in a day while men do less physical-demanding tasks. Accordingly, men produce more tangible results in the villages.
4. Although women have significant roles in agriculture and forestry, they have less access to and control of farming and forestry-based inputs and outputs. The village interviews showed the same tendency; the village women all stated that the key decisions on land and forest use are made by men.
5. Experience from other sectors, such as fisheries, shows that women's multiple roles in traditional, complex and lengthy value chains tend to diminish when value chains are formalized.

# Implications for the Planned GCF Programme

The planned GCF programme will have great leverage to advocate for improving women's representation in forest management positions from the national level to the local level. At the national level, the political will to increase the number of female Government staff in conservation work exists. A consultation with Dof and RECOTFC could help to shed more light on this issue.

At the provincial and district level, the same principles apply, but one additional barrier to travel to remote villages is that women living in remoter areas are often less confident than men. This even more relevant knowing that women living in remoter areas are often less confident than men.

The influence of the education gender gap is also very relevant to the scope of work of the planned GCF programme. Global experience shows that education has considerable power to help individuals reconsider environmentally harmful lifestyles and behaviors. Women's lower educational attainment levels are a major barrier to women's participation in forest management.

A lower education status is also often mentioned as the key reason for Lao women's low levels of confidence, which strongly effects their willingness to aim for leading positions in their communities and to be active change-makers in their families and villages. This was absolutely reinforced in the village interviews; all women groups showed high interest and willingness to participate.

# References

* World Education Blog 2013


---
**Page 158**
---

1. The planned GCF programme should employ a lead one advisor with in-depth knowledge of the sector and experience in mainstreaming in the conservation sector. The vacancy should explicitly require such expertise. To ensure sound integration into all activities, this position would ideally be with a senior technical advisor rather than with a junior position. This person would be most appropriate to have a background in environmental science or a related field and experience in mainstreaming efforts, including gender sensitization for both men and women. It is important, however, to equally empower all team members to be able to mainstream the programme into the local area, especially the senior staff and the staff responsible for monitoring and evaluation.
2. Research could be commissioned by the planned GCF programme to identify and highlight opportunities existing in forest management and decision-making that they currently do but feel insecure about their lack of education. The planned GCF programme therefore needs to support financial services, financial management and business development services. The results of this research should influence any further analysis on new potential value chains promoting deforestation-free agriculture.
3. The planned GCF programme can consider accessing information about the impact of regulations on women's family budgets, and what incentives exist to encourage women's roles as providers, and providers, including financial incentives. It is also important to identify any risk avoidance attitudes based on their current situation and to provide education levels across the country to help them understand the benefits of gender equality. Women are more likely to be interested in micro-finance, but targeted support is needed to enable them to build confidence to take on more prominent management positions in village management units.
4. It is further recommended to support gender sensitization for men and women, raising awareness about gender equality as well as the national legal and regulatory framework. In addition, it should serve as an important resource for total women providing information on key services.

8. Summary of stakeholder consultations on gender

Summary of comments and recommendations from the stakeholder consultations

In general, the programme was well received by all consulted stakeholders. At the district and village level, many participants noticed that resources are becoming increasingly scarce and unpredictable. Many women have seen the impact of deforestation and forest degradation on their livelihoods. As forests become increasingly degraded, women must either travel longer distances, or use less NTFP's (nutrient-rich fertilizers). In some villages, women have been forced to leave their homes in search of work elsewhere.

| The current Forestry Law draft and PRAPs do not reflect upon gender aspects to the land use sector                                                                                                                                                                                                               | The program will support the development and implementation of gender-sensitive regulations and guidelines, as well as implement gender sensitive policies in the content of stakeholder consultations. The program's safeguard, gender and M&E managers will review the legislation, regulations and guidelines to ensure that they are inclusive of women's rights and needs. As a result, new/revised laws, regulations and guidelines will be developed and implemented. Communication materials on forest protection, REDD+ and community engagement will be prepared for all implementing stakeholders. The program will support the development of guidelines local to different ethnic languages and develop gender-sensitive guidelines for work in different ethnic languages. The program will support the development of guidelines for women's participation in forest management and alternative pathways for people | 1.2 1.3 (5) (4) (1) (1) (1) |                                          |
| The current capacities of Government staff at all levels, to actively integrate gender and ethnic considerations into their work, are not adequately addressed. Communication materials on forest protection, REDD+ and community engagement will be prepared for all implementing stakeholders. As a result, new/revised laws, regulations and guidelines will be developed and implemented. Communication materials on forest protection, REDD+ and community engagement will be prepared for all implementing stakeholders. The program will support the development of guidelines local to different ethnic languages and develop gender-sensitive guidelines for work in different ethnic languages. The program will support the development of guidelines for women's participation in forest management and alternative pathways for people | The program will support the development of guidelines for women's participation in forest management and alternative pathways for people                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |                                              |

| Women play an important role as family leaders, and while women's decisions are often made jointly in many communities, it is not expected for the male partner to be involved in decision making. Early experiences with mistreatment of women by men will lead to a more positive attitude towards women in the future. Gender equality and social inclusion will be better achieved if women have access to finance and training. For instance, at training sessions on financial literacy, women are underrepresented, from whom important information is being disseminated. Women's participation in training sessions will improve their knowledge of financial matters and increase their confidence in managing their finances. 1. What are the main topics covered in the document?
The main topics covered in the document include forests, national parks, wildlife reserves, and buffer zone community forests; gender equality in forest management modalities; gender-sensitive REDD+ mechanisms; women's participation in law enforcement measures; and current challenges in improving tenure security for women.
2. What are the key points discussed in each section?
In the first section, the document discusses the collection of NTTFP & firewood by women in Chinese markets and their benefits. In the second section, it explores the role of women in forest management activities and the importance of gender equality in this field. The third section focuses on the assessment of current capacities to support the planned program in regards to gender equality. The fourth section discusses the implementation of REDD+ mechanisms and their integration with other policies. The fifth section examines the development of regulations and guidelines based on the new Forest Law from a gender perspective. The sixth section explores ways to include women in law enforcement measures and the importance of supporting them in this role. The seventh section discusses the current challenges in improving tenure security for women and potential solutions.
3. What are the implications of the document's findings?
The implications of the document's findings suggest that there is a need to improve gender equality in forest management modalities, support women's participation in law enforcement measures, and address current challenges in improving tenure security for women. The document also highlights the importance of integrating REDD+ mechanisms with other policies and developing regulations and guidelines based on a gender perspective.
4. What are the main arguments presented in the document?
The main arguments presented in the document include the need to improve gender equality in forest management modalities, support women's participation in law enforcement measures, and address current challenges in improving tenure security for women. The document also highlights the importance of integrating REDD+ mechanisms with other policies and developing regulations and guidelines based on a gender perspective.
5. What are the main sources cited in the document?
The main sources cited in the document include the National Treasury Bureau, the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Affairs, and various research studies and reports.

# Markets: Access to MF and Private Sector Investments

## Introduction

When creating access to MF (Microfinance Institutions), conducting educational trainings on MF which are sensitive to women's fears and insecurities regarding risk and lower education levels is crucial. This section explores the potentials and challenges of inviting private sector investments into microfinance institutions, specifically focusing on timber and fiber industries.

## How do you see the potentials and challenges in inviting private sector investments into MF?

The potentials of private sector investments in MF include increased access to capital for small businesses, job creation, and economic growth. However, there are also challenges such as ensuring fair terms and conditions for both the MF and the investors, managing risks associated with investing in emerging industries, and maintaining transparency and accountability.

## How could the GoI ensure that private sector investments in MF benefit the villagers?

To ensure that private sector investments in MF benefit the villagers, the government should consider implementing policies such as:

1. Establishing clear guidelines for investment projects, including requirements for social impact and community benefits.
2. Providing technical assistance to MF operators to help them manage risks and maximize returns.
3. Encouraging collaboration between MF operators and investors to ensure that the needs of both parties are met.
4. Implementing monitoring and evaluation systems to track the progress and impact of investment projects.
5. Providing incentives for investors who demonstrate a commitment to social responsibility and community development.

## Conclusion

Inviting private sector investments into MF can provide significant benefits to both the MF and the villagers, but it is important to address the challenges associated with such investments. By implementing policies that promote transparency, accountability, and social impact, the government can ensure that private sector investments in MF are beneficial for all stakeholders involved.

The third section focuses on the capacities of women to support the planned program in terms of gender equality and the importance of a specialized training program. The fourth section discusses the integration of REDD+ and gender aspects into the SEDP and the challenges faced by women in implementing any specific activities.

12. How can women be part in law enforcement measures?

Patrolling is accompanied by PAFO, DAFO, DOFI together with milita and police. Not possible without villagers' involvement. Villages often request Gol support. Work can be done through (hunters, kaggers). Some women might have the capacity, but not in general.

13. LUP and improved tenure security: what are the current challenges esp. for women, what needs to be improved?

LUP has majorly completed except of villages with lots of immigration which makes it difficult for authorities to update the necessary information to allocate land. Sustainable LUP needs to think more long term than it previously has. Sing District has been successful in this regard. However, there is a need for gender equality. LUP has so far benefitted everyone equally. Participation in forest-related decisions was very equal, and women and men feel equally involved.

14. Development of a National Forest Monitoring System: how can women contribute?

Women know the forests well and should be largely involved in their monitoring. They are also viewed as more reliable and honest than men in providing this kind of information.

15. Programme wants to promote deforestation-free agriculture, away from shifting cultivation practices, and promoting alternative cash crop production practices. Which implications will that have for the extension services provided? Programme will also be involved in forest-based learning and consultation activities with participating farmers/villages.

Cardamom, tea, NTFP: A negative point in order to plant cash crops, small trees are often cut down. So the balance for agrobiodiversity is not perfect, leads to decreasing biodiversity. In we amp up cash crops, this also often means disturbance of wildlife.

Shifting cultivation needs extensive labour, so any change from that to cash crops is likely positive in regards to people's work load. However, it is not clear whether this will be largely targeted, etc., also with technical knowledge to empower them, and to utilize their role as their children's educators.

16. Promotion of deforestation-free value chains and access to markets: what are women's greatest challenges to develop such value chains now, what are their barriers to access markets now?

In the promotion, it is often good to discuss potential work divisions needed for a new product (e.g. men do planting, women harvesting etc.).

14. How do you see the potentials and challenges in inviting private sector investments in village level activities?

The potentials and challenges in inviting private sector investments in village level activities are that it may lead to a situation where women are not able to participate fully in decision making processes, as they may be seen as less capable or less interested in the business. Additionally, there is a risk that the investments may not be beneficial for the villagers if they do not have the necessary skills or knowledge to manage and operate the businesses.

15. Any other remarks?

LWU should always be involved in village level activities because they can easily access village women. So they recommend us to make the responsibility very clear that LWU is committed to supporting women entrepreneurs, and we are assured that gender is always an integral part. They feel ready to fully support the projects that generate income for the villagers.

# Infrastructure Improvements for DAFO Staff

## Introduction

This document outlines the importance of infrastructure improvements as part of the program for the Department of Agriculture and Food Security (DAFO) staff. The aim is to enhance the capabilities and efficiency of DAFO staff in accessing more remote areas.

## Infrastructure Improvements

### 1. Access Roads and Pathways

- **Improved Road Conditions**: Regular maintenance and repair of roads and pathways will ensure safe and efficient travel for DAFO staff.
- **New Construction**: Building new access roads and pathways in remote areas can significantly reduce travel time and improve overall accessibility.

### 2. Communication Infrastructure

- **Enhanced Telecommunications**: Improving telecommunications infrastructure, including satellite communication, will enable better connectivity in remote areas.
- **Mobile Network Expansion**: Expanding mobile network coverage in remote areas will provide DAFO staff with reliable internet access for work and communication purposes.

### 3. Power Supply and Energy Infrastructure

- **Renewable Energy Sources**: Investing in renewable energy sources, such as solar or wind power, can reduce the reliance on non-renewable energy sources and improve the sustainability of infrastructure improvements.
- **Power Grid Upgrades**: Upgrading the power grid to accommodate increased demand from remote areas will ensure a stable and reliable supply of electricity for DAFO staff.

### 4. Water Supply Infrastructure

- **Water Treatment Facilities**: Building or upgrading water treatment facilities in remote areas can provide clean and safe drinking water for DAFO staff.
- **Water Distribution Systems**: Expanding water distribution systems to cover more remote areas will ensure that DAFO staff have access to a reliable source of water.

## Conclusion

Infrastructure improvements are crucial for the effective functioning of the Department of Agriculture and Food Security (DAFO) program. By investing in improved road conditions, communication infrastructure, power supply and energy infrastructure, and water supply infrastructure, DAFO staff can access more remote areas with greater ease and efficiency. This will ultimately enhance the capabilities and efficiency of DAFO staff, enabling them to better serve their communities and contribute to the achievement of the program's goals.

# Their dependency on rubber is too high, especially with the existing monopoly by Yunnan which already has huge concessions.

## 16. Any other remarks?

The funding support for the tourism site is ending soon, so the villagers are afraid what will happen then. The size already needs more maintenance than they can afford to provide. They also need funding and technical assistance in developing the site into a sustainable business model that can generate income from visitors.

If this is not possible, the village may have to close down. However, there are other options such as developing the site with accommodation for tourists, and other interesting features like in other sites (swing etc.).

They would also like more traders coming to the village and buy handicrafts – if that would happen regularly, many women would potentially be interested to take small loans to develop their handicraft businesses.

Agricultural practices need to be improved, and market access beyond rubber.

Watersheds are shared with other villages, so it is very polluted due to rubber pesticides. So even though villagers live at the stream, they experience severely limited water access.

Gender Action Plan (GAP): This GAP forms the basis for operationalizing the results and objectives of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Program through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management. It is closely aligned with the outputs of the top-frame and planned activities, and is considered in the programme design and during the implementation of programme measures.
2. Gender perspective: The GAP ensures effective gender mainstreaming and integration of a consistent gender perspective in the GCP programme in order to maximize climate and development coherence. It also aims to monitor and mitigate potential adverse risks over the duration of the programme, and to ensure that the programme is compliant with GCP's gender policy (GCP-G).
3. Gender-sensitive language: A gender-sensitive language is used in reports, training materials and publications. This includes using gender-neutral terms where appropriate, and avoiding stereotypes or biases.
4. Gender-sensitive forest management: The programme team(s) will consider the impact of their decisions on women and girls during the hiring process and further developed by means of training.
5. Gender-sensitive language in reports, training materials and publications: A gender-sensitive language is used in reports, training materials and publications. This includes using gender-neutral terms where appropriate, and avoiding stereotypes or biases.

# Output 3: Climate mitigation action through forestry

## Activity 3.1: Sustainable management of production forests

- **Objective:** To reduce greenhouse gas emissions and enhance carbon sequestration in forest ecosystems.
- **Activities:**
  - Implementing sustainable forest management practices, such as selective logging and reforestation.
  - Promoting the use of certified sustainably managed forests (CSMF) to ensure that forest products meet environmental standards.
  - Encouraging the adoption of agroforestry systems that integrate trees with crops and livestock, enhancing biodiversity and carbon sequestration.
- **Monitoring:** Regularly assessing the impact of these activities on greenhouse gas emissions and forest health.

## Activity 3.2: Sustainable management of production forests

- **Objective:** To reduce deforestation and promote reforestation in forest ecosystems.
- **Activities:**
  - Implementing policies that encourage the conservation of existing forests and the restoration of degraded areas.
  - Promoting the use of certified sustainably managed forests (CSMF) to ensure that forest products meet environmental standards.
  - Encouraging the adoption of agroforestry systems that integrate trees with crops and livestock, enhancing biodiversity and carbon sequestration.
- **Monitoring:** Regularly assessing the impact of these activities on deforestation rates and forest health.

## Output 4: Programme management, coordination, monitoring and reporting

- **Objective:** To ensure that climate mitigation actions through forestry are carried out effectively and efficiently.
- **Activities:**
  - Establishing a programme office to oversee the implementation of climate mitigation actions through forestry.
  - Coordinating with other government departments, NGOs, and private sector organizations to ensure that all stakeholders are working towards the same goals.
  - Monitoring progress towards the objectives outlined in Output 3 and reporting on results to relevant stakeholders.
- **Monitoring:** Regularly reviewing the effectiveness of climate mitigation actions through forestry and making necessary adjustments to improve outcomes.

1. **Identify the Document Type**: Determine if the document is a report, proposal, or any other type of textual document. This will help you understand the structure and content of the document.

2. **Read the Document Carefully**: Read through the document to get an understanding of its main points, arguments, and data. Pay attention to headings, subheadings, bullet points, and tables that might provide structured information.

3. **Transcribe the Content**: Use a text editor or word processor to transcribe the content of the document into Markdown format. Here's how you can do it:

   - **Links**: If the document contains links, you can use `[link text](URL)` to create a clickable link in Markdown.

4. **Review and Edit**: Review your transcription for accuracy and clarity. Make sure that all headings, subheadings, bullet points, tables, and links are correctly formatted and linked.

5. **Save the Document**: Save your Markdown document with a `.md` extension (e.g., `project_1-level_gender_action_plan.md`).

# Gender Action Plan: Monitoring, evaluation and reporting

## Introduction

A long-term safeguard, gender and M&E specialist as well as a national expert who will support overseer, guide and coordinate gender-related measures within the programme, and ensure that the GAP is monitored, evaluated and reported for the GAP. They will be responsible for monitoring, evaluation and reporting to the GAP.

## Responsibilities

* Liaison with all program stakeholders
* Responsibility for overseeing program communication and stakeholder engagement on topics related to gender
* Development of program documents/publications/ guidelines/ policies to ensure effective gender mainstreaming, and that information is gender-sensitive
* Close cooperation with the LWU and LFNC on topics related to gender, building on best practices
* Dissemination of information about the grievance mechanism to program partners, local communities, CSOs, among others.
* Monitoring progress, including in achieving the ESMP and gender action plan targets, and ensuring adaptive management (as needed).
* Monitoring program progress, including in achieving the ESMP and gender action plan targets, and ensuring adaptive management (as needed).
